12/10/25, 4:45 PM - Gurmeet Singh Manku: My strategy revolves around these preparations:
1. Fruity Oatmeal: rolled oats (soaked in water, overnight preferred, optionally microwaved in the morning). Add freshly chopped fruits (banana, apple, persimmon, …) and berries (blueberries, raspberries, amla powder, …). We may add overnight soaked dried fruits like dried apricots, prunes, dried pineapple, … and dried berries (dried golden berries, dried goji berries, …) along with spice mix (cardamom powder, cinnamon powder, cacao powder, …). <— Very simple to make! I carry this on flights (domestic, international) too!
2. ⁠Sweet potatoes + canned beans + salsa: Almost all hotels & AirBnb’s have a microwave. So I microwave some sweet potatoes in their whole form. I slice them after they are cooked (steamed). Add canned beans (garbanzo or black beans or red kidney beans), along with simple salsa: tomato + onion + cilantro + jalapeno + lemon + spice mix (All Purpose Seasoning or Trader Joe’s 21 Salute Seasoning, for example). If I have time, I will also add cucumber, tomatillo, sliced grapes, etc. I also add overnight soaked dried berry medley to enhance the flavor.
3. ⁠Romaine lettuce — just wash it and eat it raw — as it is (without chopping). Or chop it and add to the sweet potato + canned beans + salsa preparation above. <This message was edited>
12/10/25, 4:47 PM - +1 (415) 706-2250: I recently went to LA and on a short cruise. I deliberately chose to drive instead of fly to have more flexibility and autonomy. I spent hours emptying my fridge making an everything but the sink salad and steamed vegetable dishes. They lasted for two days. I used Happy Cow app to find vegan restaurants in the area. I was lucky and found a German restaurant with great vegan options nearby. On the cruise, I looked for vegan options, often sides, and had support from two cooks who prepared me special dishes with tofu and veggies. Fruit was available in abundance. 
I couldn't avoid salt, oil, sugar and felt the consequences. Returning was challenging and took two weeks to readjust.

12/12/25, 11:00 AM - +1 (510) 541-2702: One trick I randomly discovered to circumvent cooking/boiling steel cut oats (and thus saving time) is to soak them in warm/hot liquid overnight. They turn out slightly chewy, which I actually like more than soft.
For 1/3 cup oats, I add ~2/3 cup liquid, microwave on full power for 1.5 mins, cover and rest. Microwave again for 1 min after an hour or so, cover and rest overnight.

One small onion finely chopped 
1-2 small or medium size tomatoes - finely chopped 
1/2 cup finely chopped cilantro 
Add 1 large tablespoon powdered mixture of sesame seeds and roasted peanut.
I heaping teaspoon of besan 
Mix everything together nicely and then add powder masala like turmeric, coriander powder, red chilli powder, goda masala and hing 
Mix again and add some water- I added 1/2 cup water 
Just add the thick sliced Karela which is slight precooked in microwave for 3 mins 

Mix everything and add in pressure cooker 
I cooked until 2 whistles on medium flame. 
Add a little more water if needed, my curry got a little burnt at the bottom.
After done, add date powder and lime juice and sprinkle chaat masala <This message was edited>

12/22/25, 6:37 PM - Shalini Other Lady WFPB: Love the presentation ❤️ can you share how you make the dhoklis? They look amazing but different from what I make.
12/22/25, 7:12 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: 
12/22/25, 7:12 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: <Media omitted>

12/22/25, 7:12 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: <Media omitted>
12/22/25, 7:12 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: *Rajasthani-style Dal Dhokli* 😋

For the dhokli: I took 1 cup whole wheat flour, 1/3 cup besan, 3-4 tbsp fennel seeds, turmeric powder, coriander powder, red chili powder, asafetida, and a pinch of salt. I kneaded the dough by gradually adding warm water, about 4-5 fl oz in total. The dough was a bit wet (cos besan absorbs some water when rested). I covered the container with a lid and let the dough rest for 20 mins. When I removed the lid, the dough was no longer wet. I then split the dough into three pieces and rolled them. The part that takes time for me is: from each of them, I’d take a small bite-size piece and work with index fingers and thumb of both hands to make it flat and circular. Place them on a plate so that they don’t stick to each other. To save on time and effort, a) I’ve seen videos where ppl roll it with a flour pin (like a roti), and run a pizza cutter to make diamond-like shapes, or, b) you could use a bottle cap (or, a small-sized cookie cutter mold), or anything that helps you get a circular or any shape that you prefer. In the past, when I’d not want to put in the effort to make dhoklis, I’d use pasta. :) 

While the dough was resting, I soaked 1/2 cup green split moong dal, 1/2 cup black split moong dal, and 1/3 cup chana dal, for about 15 mins, and pressure cooked for 3 whistles with turmeric powder and asafetida. After opening the lid, I turned on the stove, added 3-4 cups water to the dal, let it reach a boil. At this point, I gradually added one dhokli after another to the cooker. Do not add them all together (or, you’ll risk them all attaching together). Let it boil with a medium flame and allow for the dhokli to reach the top of the cooker. Gently stir it every once in a while to unsettle the dhoklis that have settled together at the bottom, and remove the foam/froth that forms. Several regions of Rajasthan use different dals with varied proportions of the dals. 

On the side, to a sauce pan, I added 2 tsp mustard and cumin seeds each, chopped and added each of these: 1 red onion, 8 garlic cloves, 5 green chillies, 1 inch ginger, and 2 tomatoes, with turmeric powder, coriander powder, red chili powder. I water sautéed them all. Also, chop 1 more small red onion for serving it later as a topping. You may adjust the quantities of these as per your liking. 

When almost all of the dhoklis have reached the top (just like pasta does!), I added the contents of the sauce pan to the cooker with dal and dhokli, and let it simmer for about 5-10 mins for the flavors to blend in. I then, added salt to taste, and red chili powder to give it a distinctive tadka like color. You may add a pinch or two of garam masala — I did not add it. Stirred it, turned off the stove, and topped it with cilantro. With every serving, I topped it with lemon juice and chopped red onions. Traditionally, it is also cherished with a red spicy garlic chutney, but since, we avoid ghee/oil, the spicy chutney may be skipped. WFPBNO. <This message was edited>

1/4/26, 12:38 PM - Ashwini Vikram: Tarun, I simply soaked for about 6 hours 1 cup of whole green gram and 1/2 cup of whole black gram with a tsp of methi seeds. I then ground them separately. 
Mix together and let them ferment overnight.
No rice at all. Please use very little water while grinding. It's impossible for it to look like an Idli if not. Also steam them longer than regular white Idlis.

1/5/26, 7:38 PM - Kusum WFPB: I have a cast iron grill pan which I heat it on high to get good grill mark and then lower them slight cook the pineapples not over cook them 
For the eggplant roundels, sprinkle salt lightly and leave it on the cutting board for an hour or so, you will see the eggplants are releasing water.
For the coating, I used 1 toasted ezikel bread and grind to a coarse bread crumbs, add powder spices like smoked paprika, garlic powder, turmeric, black pepper, amchur powder and some chaat masala, mix all of it well and then spread the powder in a plate and coat all eggplant slices and bake in oven at 350 for 30 mins, turning then once half way through. 
In the past I noticed that if I skip salting the eggplants, they don’t retain their shape 
I have also tried cooking in a pan but frequently turning them they break or don’t cook well 
I prefer baking in oven. <This message was edited>

1/8/26, 8:42 PM - Gurmeet: I had eyeballed all the quantities.  It is one such recipe where you wake up, have just a few minutes to come up with a recipe, put a few ingredients together and wish that it turns out good:) I put together
Whole wheat flour- 1 cup approx 
Rajgeera flour- 1/4 cup
Wheat germ- 2 tbls
Flax eggs- 1 tbls flaxseed mixed in with some water
Baking powder and baking soda- 1 tsp each 
Vanilla essence powder- 2 tsp
Cinnamon powder- to taste
Pumpkin spice- to taste
Water- Add water and mix everything together to make a pancake consistency batter.

1/10/26, 5:41 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: What are simple meals!? Repetition makes it simple. The same meal or the prep style that’d seem difficult 30 meals ago, when repeated over and again, will seem simple 30 repetitions later. 

On spices, most of us (esp. incl. me), just don’t understand them well. We do not know how much to use? With which ingredients to pair them? In what form to use? How does the flavor composition change with different veggies? How much to cook them? Etc. Our unfamiliarity drifts us towards either avoiding them, or relying on someone else’s packaging for us (predefined seasonings, spice mixes, or recipes with measured amts of spices). Unfortunately, several of these, work well with familiar ingredients (think: chole masala with chickpeas, tomatoes and onions, or, taco seasoning with well-known taco ingredients). With newer ingredients (say, you try to purchase and prep a recipe with radicchio and fava beans for the first time), it is hard to gauge, right!? So, we turn towards avoiding them (spices). Instead, we rely on familiar and uncomplicated pairings: black pepper, salt, lemon juice, etc. — where flavor attributions are distinct and well-adapted to our flavor profiles. Similar conclusions can be drawn for leafy greens, a variety of veggies, fruits, whole grains, beans, lentils, legumes, etc. 

We are likely lazy too: in the sense that we do not take time to experiment with newer ingredients and/or just do not have the interest for it, and would rather choose to do sth else instead. Those that do have the interest and take the time for it, gravitate towards a career of sorts with it (chefs, books, shows, etc.).

The way I see it: continue to be curious, and be experimental. We never know what becomes a favorite, is on a repetition for us, and elevates it to be simple. I am sure, Gurmeet wouldn’t have found out that he loves Teff grain with fruits, cacao powder, etc. as one of his favorites and simplest meals to cherish unless he had such an approach. Most ppl that haven’t tried it, would be like: that’s alien and sounds complicated. <This message was edited>

1/12/26, 9:47 AM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: Teff is a tiny whole grain (technically: a grass, I think, like millets, and wild rice). Injera (ofted eaten at Ethiopian cuisine) is a fermented teff crepe, similar to a dosa. <This message was edited>
1/12/26, 9:49 AM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: <Media omitted>
1/12/26, 9:50 AM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: <Media omitted>
1/12/26, 9:53 AM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: How do I cook teff? 1/2 cup raw teff, boil it with 2 cups of water for 12-15 mins on medium flame. Lid not needed. Stir every 4-5 mins. Towards the end, add cacao powder and mix it well. At times, I’ll add unsweetened soy milk. Top it with fruits. :)

1/12/26, 4:57 PM - +1 (650) 248-1927: Nice. How do you make the chutney?
1/12/26, 5:38 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: Each time it is a bit different depending on what I have on hand and what I remember to add. Broadly, 1 bunch cilantro, 1/2 bunch mint (at times, with stems), 1/2 to 1 inch ginger, several green chillies (depending on how spicy I’d want it). For most days, this suffices. Optional ingredients: garlic (for flavor), green peas (for the chutney to retain the green color and make it a bit thicker), roasted split chana dal (for the thickness, and I’d compliment it with asafetida), black pepper (for a little spicy flavor), fennel seeds (for flavor), 1/2 cucumber with skin (for a different texture), walnuts (for creaminess), dried fruits (when I need it to be a bit sweeter: think raisins, dried apricots), etc. Blend them with water (less: when I want it like a spread, more: if I want it like a dressing). At times, I’d even add other herbs (rosemary, oregano, thyme, etc.) with their stems, and/or leafy green stems (whatever I have). I always top it with lemon juice. Occasionally, I’ll add a bit chaat masala, raw mango powder (amchur), or sth like it. <This message was edited>

1/15/26, 1:23 PM - Gurmeet: Thanks so much for asking the question about disposing off of the kit. I looked it up and here is the information. 
"To discard a mushroom growing kit, you must separate the organic growing medium (substrate) from its packaging. The substrate can be composted or used as a garden amendment, while the packaging components (plastic bag, cardboard box) should be disposed of according to local recycling and waste guidelines. 
Disposing of the Organic Substrate
The spent substrate is rich in nutrients and is excellent for enriching soil. 
Composting Break up the substrate into pieces and add it to your home compost bin or worm farm. The organic material will decompose over time.
Garden Mulch Scatter the broken-up substrate directly onto your garden beds or around potted plants to act as a nutrient-rich mulch. You may even find bonus mushrooms growing from the residual mycelium after rain.
Outdoor Inoculation For some species, you can try burying the block in a shaded area of your garden to encourage further natural flushes of growth. Check specific guidance for your species; for example, lion's mane kits use strains that could out-compete some wild UK varieties, so proper disposal is essential to protect local ecosystems. 
Disposing of the Packaging
After removing the substrate, the remaining packaging should be handled as follows:
Plastic Liner Bag The thick plastic bag the substrate came in is typically not curbside recyclable due to its material and use. Dispose of it in your general waste bin (landfill).
Cardboard Box The outer cardboard box can be flattened and placed in your household recycling bin. 
Special Considerations for Contaminated Kits
If your kit has developed unusual green, black, or red molds (like trichoderma) that are not part of the intended growth, take extra care to contain the spores. 
Spray the contaminated area with a bleach solution or Lysol before handling to minimize the spread of unwanted spores into your home or garden.
Seal the entire contaminated block in a plastic bag before placing it in the general garbage bin. Avoid composting it to prevent introducing the contaminant to your garden.
Thoroughly clean any surfaces, tubs, or tools the contaminated kit touched with a disinfectant like 70% rubbing alcohol or a bleach solution. "

1/17/26, 5:28 PM - +1 (650) 906-1533: Kale holds the best in a fridge as it’s a tough leafy green. I do a combination of Kalie, Swiss chard and Arugula most often. 

Quick version of Broccoli Rabe + Fava beans sauté. Super simple recipe:

-soak Fava beans overnight
-chop up the Rabe (one inch pieces is my ideal)
-on a pan, saute the Rabe with water. If Fava beans are on the tougher side after soaking, start with it first. Add the remaining ingredient + salt (light). 
-top it with any crunchy - toasted seeds or nuts. 

Methi sambar:

-finely chop up cleaned methi leaves (if stem is tough, cut off a few inches off the bottom. Once you get used to eating them, the toughness will hopefully not bother you. It happened to me)
-cook dals (I use a mix of Chana(yellow split), toor and moong dals) with turmeric
-take a pot that is big enough to make sambar (which will be a stew so you need 3-4x the volume of dal). Saute the chopped up methi leaves in water. Add salt if you like. Once they get tender, add tamarind water. Use a small amount first, say, half the size of a golf ball.  Let the methi cook half way through. Add sambar masala powder and cook another 5-8 minutes. Add the cooked dal and let it simmer until it bubbles up. Add dry tadka. I use mustard seeds, asafoetida, small red chili and curry leaves. 
-enjoy with brown rice or quinoa.

1/26/26, 6:40 PM - Ashwini Vikram: Asparagus chutney

Main Ingredients:
Steamed  asparagus, green+dried red chilli, tamarind, garlic as desired.

For the crunch: 
Channa dal, Urad dal, Sesame seeds, Peanuts - pick and choose whichever or a tsp of all of these.

Dry roast crunchy ingredients and then add garlic and chillies and roast some more. Now add the steamed asparagus and combine and blend together.

Season with mustard seeds, curry leaves and asafoerida. <This message was edited>

1/28/26, 9:07 AM - Gurmeet: Asked chatgpt here's the response "Yep — those white spots are almost certainly OK 👍
On sun-dried figs, those white specks are usually natural sugar crystals (often called sugar bloom). As figs dry, their natural sugars migrate to the surface and crystallize. Totally normal, very common, and safe to eat.
How to double-check it’s sugar and not mold:
✅ Sugar crystals:
Look dry, chalky, or grainy
Evenly scattered
Hard or slightly gritty if you rub them
No off smell
🚫 Mold (not safe):
Fuzzy or hairy
Blue, green, or gray patches
Musty or sour smell
Soft or slimy texture underneath
From your photo, these look dry, uniform, and non-fuzzy, which is classic sugar crystallization — especially common in organic, unsulfured figs like these.
Tip: If the texture bugs you, you can soak the figs in warm water for 5–10 minutes and the crystals will dissolve.
You’re good to enjoy them 🌿🍯"

1/31/26, 11:18 AM - +1 (510) 304-4426: You can presoak the daals for some time and boil them instead of pressure cooking.  Does not take much longer and I believe the taste is a tad better.  The daals are not exposed to higher temperatures.  Inside the pressure cooker it get a bit hotter than boiling - which leads to faster cooking, but then the food is exposed to higher temperatures that some say is not good for some nutrients (I have no data to support this claim but I do sense a difference in taste).

1/31/26, 7:40 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: Many of us are used to placing roti/rice on the large portion of the plate, and a salad rarely has a place, or at times, is served on a side plate. I like this plate where we keep a greens, beet, and cruciferous veggie salad on the large portion of our main plate, and position our whole grain roti/rice on the side plate. Several dining etiquettes and/or restaurants also serve it this way, but such plates, generally lack a good veggie salad. Thanks for sharing! <This message was edited>

2/2/26, 5:30 AM - +1 (650) 248-1927: We used neither and it was still delicious. Below is our version:
The Curry Miso (Swami Sauce):
1. Add to blender: 2 tbsp Miso, 1 tbsp Maple Syrup, 1 tbsp Lemon Juice, 1 tsp Curry Powder, 1 clove Garlic, 1-2 tbsp Water.
2. Blend until smooth.
2/2/26, 5:32 AM - +1 (650) 248-1927: I also made another sauce (similarly inspired) which went very well with tofu.
The Ginger Nama (Green Goddess Sauce):
1. Add to blender: 1/3 cup chopped Ginger, 4 cloves Garlic, 1/2 cup Lemon Juice, 1/3 cup Tamari, 2 tbsp Tahini.
2. Blend on high.
3. Stream in 1/4 cup water until it pours like dressing.

2/8/26, 10:34 PM - Gurmeet: Turban Squash halwa
Turban squash - 2 1/2 cups pulp( I steamed it in the microwave and scooped the pulp)
Oats milk- 11/2 cups ( we may use plant milk of our choice)
Dates- 10-12 soaked in water and then mashed.
Dried figs- 6-7 soaked in water and then mashed.
Raisins- mixed variety( green, red, black)
Cardamom powder
Nutmeg powder
All spice
Cinnamon powder
Method-
1. In a pot add the turban squash pulp, oat milk, mashed dates, mashed dried figs, and soaked raisins. Bring this to a boil. 
2. Lower the heat, cover the pot and cook for 20 minutes stirring intermittently. The mix bubbles and splutters.
3. Stir with lid on until the halwa thickens for 20-25 minutes. 
4. Add the cinnamon nutmeg, and all spice powders, mix everything well.
5. Take the lid off, stir the halwa consistently until it thickens. It took another 10-12 minutes. 
6. Halwa is ready once it begins to stick to the bottom of the pan. 
5. Enjoy!! 

Notes- 1. We may garnish with slivered almonds, and dehydrated fruits of our choice.
2. Almond flour may be added.

2/10/26, 9:18 PM - Gurmeet Singh Manku: I got accustomed to adding all of these together in the Fruity Oatmeal I prepare. My preparation has a LOT of fruits & berries, and only a small amount of oats. A medley of Fruits & Berries are super flavorful, and allows for introducing all of these in small amounts:
* Ginger powder
* ⁠Cardamom powder
* ⁠Cinnamon powder
* ⁠Nutmeg
* ⁠Star Anise
* ⁠Allspice
* ⁠Black pepper
* ⁠Vanilla extract
* ⁠Matcha powder
* ⁠Clove powder
* Fennel powder
My taste buds have adjusted over time to start enjoying all of the above in Fruity Oatmeal.

Additionally, we can experiment with small quantities (1/8 tsp) of a variety of berry powders and fruit powders and mushroom powders and misc powders if we feel like:
* Amla powder
* ⁠Blackcurrant powder
* ⁠Tart cherry powder
* ⁠Sour grape powder
* ⁠Baobab powder
* ⁠Camu camu powder
* ⁠Black goji berry powder
* ⁠Acai berry powder
* ⁠Pomegranate powder
* ⁠Sumac powder
* ⁠⁠Dried fig powder
* ⁠…
* ⁠Maca powder
* ⁠Mesquite powder
* ⁠…
* ⁠Mushroom powders of various types (in small quantities)

If Fruity Oatmeal is dominated by Fruits & Berries (and less oats), then a variety of such powders (in small quantities) can be added while still tasting great.

We can also try adding a small quantity of Masala Chai Powder to Fruity Oatmeal. <This message was edited>

2/11/26, 12:46 PM - Ankit Dangi WFPB: Yes, this khichdi is cooked in a pressure cooker with seeds and spices. At times, I’d use an instant pot or cook them stovetop. In this combination, each grain and beans is equal proportion: 1/4 cup raw, soaked overnight, and pressure cooked. In parallel, I prepared the rainbow greens, veggies, and fruits salad. I don’t have a good rule of thumb here, other than: choose a variety of them by color (of the whole grains, beans, lentils, dals). Each day is a diff. combination, ratios, and colors — keeps it interesting and flavorful. <This message was edited>

2/13/26, 9:56 AM - Alpesh WFPB: I just boil 8-10 cups of water and then add diced onions, garlic, celery, carrot and tomato. Cover the pot and let it simmer for 30-45 mins. Strain the water and you have vegetable broth. Depending on what I am making I switch vegetables. If I want umami flavor, I add mushrooms. If I am making asian curry or soup, I add ginger and green onion. There are endless combinations like adding mint, cilantro, parsley, basil etc.

If I want thick soup, I just blend everything and you have a nice base which can be used in Chili or any other stew type of soups.

You can also make lot more and store it in an airtight container in the fridge for a week or so. <This message was edited>

2/15/26, 8:23 PM - Gurmeet: This cake looks so decadent 🍓🍰
2/15/26, 8:31 PM - Kumar Natarajan Satvic: Rich Chocolate cake with chocolate frosting and Strawberries:

Dry mixture: Add 1 cup oat flour, 1/2 cup almond flour, 1/2 cup cacao powder, 2 tsp baking powder and 1 tsp vanilla powder to a mixing bowl and mix well

Wet mixture: Add 1.5 cup deglet dates, 1 tsp apple cider vinegar and 1 cup soy milk to a blender and blend smooth

Pour the wet mixture into the dry mixture and add 1 more cup of soy milk (total 2 cups of soy milk) and mix well. Then bake in a small round pan for 20 min at 350 F. Once it is out of the oven, prepare the frosting 

Frosting: Soak 4-5 dates in 1/2 cup hotwater for few minutes. Then blend the dates, 1 tbsp almond flour, 1 tbsp cacao powder, 1/2 tsp vanilla powder all together until very smooth, as we will be pouring this over the cake

Pour the frosting on the cake and even it out. Add some inverted strawberries on top!  

Cake ingredients:
1 cup oat flour
1/2 cup almond flour
1/2 cup cacao powder
2 tsp baking powder
1.5 cups deglet dates
1 tsp vanilla powder or vanilla extract
1 tsp ACV
2 cups soy milk unsweetened
Several strawberries


Frosting ingredients:
4-5 deglet dates
1 tbsp almond flour
1 tbsp cacao powder
1/2 tsp vanilla powder or extract
1/2 cup hot water

2/17/26, 9:43 PM - Gurmeet: I was craving kadu borani yesterday..lol. can you please share the recipe.
2/17/26, 9:44 PM - Gurmeet: Amazing!
2/17/26, 9:57 PM - Shalini Other Lady WFPB: Sure. 

Afghan Kadu Borani

3 cloves Garlic
1 tsp Ginger
1 whole butternut squash chopped into bite size pieces
1/2 onion
1/4 tsp Black pepper, freshly ground
1 tsp Turmeric, ground
2 tsp powdered date sugar or date syrup
Lemon/lime juice to taste
Salt to taste 
Mint and cilantro to garnish

Yogurt sauce - 
1 cup soy yogurt + finely chopped ginger, garlic, mint, green chilli and cilantro + black pepper and a pinch of salt. 

Spread butternut squash in a baking tray and air fry or bake in a toaster oven for 20-25 minutes at 400until it begins to caramelise (this brings out the sugars a bit). Microwave 1/2 chopped onion for 3 minutes. Saute onion, garlic, ginger with 2 tablespoons of water for a couple of minutes. Add the spices and pumpkin. Add a little water, cover and allow the squash to soften slightly. Take out in the serving dish, add the yogurt sauce, some lemon/lime juice and chopped cilantro and mint on top.

2/19/26, 5:37 PM - Gurmeet: Rich Chocolate cake with chocolate frosting and Strawberries:

Dry mixture: Add 1 cup oat flour, 1/2 cup almond flour, 1/2 cup cacao powder, 2 tsp baking powder and 1 tsp vanilla powder to a mixing bowl and mix well

Wet mixture: Add 1.5 cup deglet dates, 1 tsp apple cider vinegar and 1 cup soy milk to a blender and blend smooth

Pour the wet mixture into the dry mixture and add 1 more cup of soy milk (total 2 cups of soy milk) and mix well. Then bake in a small round pan for 20 min at 350 F. Once it is out of the oven, prepare the frosting 

Frosting: Soak 4-5 dates in 1/2 cup hotwater for few minutes. Then blend the dates, 1 tbsp almond flour, 1 tbsp cacao powder, 1/2 tsp vanilla powder all together until very smooth, as we will be pouring this over the cake

Pour the frosting on the cake and even it out. Add some inverted strawberries on top!  

Cake ingredients:
1 cup oat flour
1/2 cup almond flour
1/2 cup cacao powder
2 tsp baking powder
1.5 cups deglet dates
1 tsp vanilla powder or vanilla extract
1 tsp ACV
2 cups soy milk unsweetened
Several strawberries


Frosting ingredients:
4-5 deglet dates
1 tbsp almond flour
1 tbsp cacao powder
1/2 tsp vanilla powder or extract
1/2 cup hot water

2/22/26, 2:30 AM - Gunjan Sahni WFPB: Choclate cake

100 gms soft boiled sweet potato
100 gms oat flour
50 gms ragi flour
70 gms deseeded dates
15-20 gms cocoa
1 tbsp vinegar
1/4 tsp baking soda
1 tsp baking powder 
Pinch of salt
Vanilla extract 

Add every thing to a high speed blender 
Pour 100 ml water and blend
You might require a little more water

Blend it to cake batter consistency 

Bake in a preheated over for 40min at 180
2/22/26, 2:30 AM - Gunjan Sahni WFPB: For frosting 

100 gms soft boiled sweet potato
40 gms nut butter (cashew/peanut/almond all works)
50 gms deseeded dates
15 gms cocoa 
Pinch of salt
Vanilla extract 
50 ml water 

Blend it all in a high speed blender 
You might require a little more water
Let the frosting be a little thin as it is easy to spread and once you refrigerate it tends to thicken a little

2/22/26, 6:48 PM - +1 (978) 266-2660: Baked tofu 

Preheat oven 400*F.     
16 oz Organic super firm Tofu
3 Tblsp Ume Plum Vinegar
1 Tblsp Tamari 
1 tsp hot sauce with garlic 
1 Tblsp rice flour 

Pat dry tofu with aid of tofu press or towel.  Once dry cut tofu into 16 slices .  Mix seasoning together - marinate tofu and on grease proof paper place individual slices of tofu sprinkle rice flour and bake for 20’minutes in preheated oven turning once after 10 minutes

2/22/26, 7:20 PM - Gurmeet: <Media omitted>
2/22/26, 7:25 PM - Gurmeet: @⁨Jasmine Satvic⁩ 
Leek and sweet potato soup ingredients 

2 large sweet potatoes, peeled & cubed
2 leeks (white + light green parts), thinly sliced
2 cloves garlic, minced
3–4 cups vegetable broth
½ tsp cumin (optional, adds warmth)
¼ tsp black pepper
Salt to taste
½ cup oat milk (optional for creaminess)
Method 
1. Sauté leeks for 5–7 minutes until soft (don’t brown).
2.  Add garlic and cook 30 seconds.
3. Add sweet potatoes, cumin, pepper, and broth.
4. Bring to boil → simmer 15–20 minutes until potatoes are soft.
5. Blend until smooth.
6. Stir in oat milk if using
